the zeroth input coefficient.  \nolinkurl{SaddleLogExpansionPowerSeries.lean}
then identifies the recursive logarithm with Mathlib's
\nolinkurl{PowerSeries.logOf}.

\nolinkurl{FabiusSecondSaddleCorrection.lean} computes \(d_2\) explicitly as a
finite polynomial in the first four bounded exponent jets, and
\nolinkurl{FabiusSecondSaddleExpansion.lean} substitutes it into the public
three-term statement:
\[
  \log F(x)=\operatorname{Main}(x)
   +\frac{A_1(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)}
   +\frac{A_2(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)^2}
   +O\!\left(\lambda(x)^{-3}\right).
\]
Both corrections are explicit continuous period-one functions; later
regularity results may provide stronger smoothness where stated.  The theorem
does not assert that either correction is nowhere zero.

\subsection{Transfer to \texorpdfstring{\(F(x)\)}{F(x)}}
\nolinkurl{FabiusFullAsymptoticExpansion.lean} inserts the logarithmic mass expansion into the exact saddle reduction, adds the periodic and explicit main terms, and absorbs the exponentially small forward tail.  The result is an all-order logarithmic expansion for \(F(x)\) at zero.

The primary remainder is \(O(\lambda^{-N})\).  A weaker but simpler corollary uses \(\lambda(x)\asymp-\log x\) to obtain
\[
  O\bigl((-\log x)^{-N}\bigr).
\]

\subsection{First-order and Wikipedia-facing corollaries}
Modules with names such as \nolinkurl{FabiusWikipediaMain.lean} and \nolinkurl{FabiusWikipediaExpansion.lean} package concise formulas suitable for comparison with public summaries.  They separate what is correct at leading order from what is false at the claimed next order.  The obstruction module demonstrates that a nonconstant dyadic-periodic correction cannot be absorbed into a vanishing error.

\subsection{The proof-audit role of the asymptotic aggregator}
\nolinkurl{PaperFabiusAsymptotic.lean} is not just a theorem list.  Its header records which informal claims are supported, which need corrected domains or periodic terms, and which are disproved.  The formal development therefore functions as an audit trail:
\begin{itemize}
  \item coarse log-squared behavior is validated;
  \item the exact delay relation is restricted to its true domain;
  \item a claimed overly small residual is refuted;
  \item the periodic Lambert-phase formula is proved independently through transforms and saddles; and
  \item arbitrary-order corrections are established at the exact phase.
\end{itemize}

\section{Legendre expansions and polynomial approximation}\label{ch:legendre}

\subsection{Why Legendre polynomials are a natural basis}
The up-function is supported on \([-1,1]\), is even, and is infinitely differentiable.  Legendre polynomials are orthogonal on exactly this interval with respect to Lebesgue measure, so they provide a canonical spectral expansion:
\[
  \RvachevUp(x)=\sum_{n\geq0}a_nP_n(x).
\]
Evenness forces every odd coefficient to vanish.  The repository does not stop at a formal \(L^2\) expansion: it evaluates the even coefficients exactly through moments, proves absolute and uniform convergence, translates the series back to the global Fabius function, and proves least-squares optimality of finite truncations.

\subsection{Building the polynomial basis}
\nolinkurl{LegendrePolynomial.lean} develops the required Legendre facts in the normalization used by the project.  The polynomials are defined through Rodrigues' formula or an equivalent derivative representation:
\[
  P_n(x)=\frac{1}{2^n n!}
          \frac{\Differential^n}{\Differential x^n}(x^2-1)^n.
\]
The module proves degree, parity, endpoint values, differential equation, and derivative bounds.

A bespoke development is useful because later coefficient formulas require explicit monomial coefficients.  A black-box orthogonal-polynomial theorem would provide orthogonality but not necessarily the exact finite sum compatible with the moment sequence.

\subsection{Sturm--Liouville orthogonality}
The Legendre differential equation is
\[
  \bigl((1-x^2)P_n'(x)\bigr)'+n(n+1)P_n(x)=0.
\]
Multiplying the equation for \(P_n\) by \(P_m\), the equation for \(P_m\) by \(P_n\), subtracting, and integrating gives
\[
  \bigl(n(n+1)-m(m+1)\bigr)
  \int_{-1}^{1}P_n(x)P_m(x)\,\Differential x=0.
\]
Boundary terms vanish because of the factor \(1-x^2\).  For \(m\ne n\), the eigenvalues differ, proving orthogonality.

\nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreSeries.lean} carries out this argument using interval integration by parts.  The proof explicitly verifies differentiability of the polynomial expressions and endpoint vanishing.  The eigenvalue inequality is discharged in natural arithmetic and cast to reals only at the final multiplication step.

\subsection{Norms and pointwise bounds}
The exact norm is
\[
  \int_{-1}^{1}P_n(x)^2\,\Differential x=\frac{2}{2n+1}.
\]
The source derives it from Rodrigues' formula and repeated integration by parts, or from a recurrence plus normalization depending on the helper theorem used.  This norm fixes the Fourier--Legendre coefficient:
\[
  a_n=\frac{2n+1}{2}
      \int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(x)P_n(x)\,\Differential x.
\]

A bound \(|P_n(x)|\leq1\) on \([-1,1]\) is proved from the differential equation and an extremum argument.  This simple uniform bound is the last ingredient needed to convert absolute convergence of coefficients into uniform convergence of the function series.

\subsection{Vanishing of odd coefficients}
The parity theorem states
\[
  P_n(-x)=(-1)^nP_n(x).
\]
Since \(\RvachevUp\) is even, the integrand \(\RvachevUp(x)P_n(x)\) is odd when \(n\) is odd.  A symmetric-interval integral of an odd integrable function is zero.  In Lean, the proof either invokes a generic odd-integral lemma or performs the substitution \(x\mapsto-x\) and adds the two integral identities.

The resulting series is indexed naturally by \(P_{2n}\).  This is not only cosmetic: the exact coefficient formula then uses the even moment \(M_{2k}\), for which the arithmetic normalization and denominator theory are available.

\subsection{Exact coefficient evaluation}
\nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreCoefficients.lean} expands \(P_{2n}\) in monomials:
\[
  P_{2n}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}c_{n,k}x^{2k}.
\]
Finite linearity of the integral gives
\[
  a_{2n}=\frac{4n+1}{2}
          \sum_{k=0}^{n}c_{n,k}M_{2k}.
\]
Every \(M_{2k}\) is the exact rational \nolinkurl{moment} value, so \(a_{2n}\) is rational.  The source also substitutes formulas expressing moments through inverse-power Fabius values, producing a coefficient formula entirely in terms of the exact dyadic table.

The proof is layered:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item establish the explicit polynomial coefficient theorem;
  \item turn polynomial evaluation into a finite sum;
  \item commute the finite sum with the integral;
  \item replace each monomial integral by the analytic moment;
  \item identify the moment with the exact rational sequence; and
  \item simplify casts and normalizations.
\end{enumerate}
At no point is an infinite series exchanged with the integral during coefficient evaluation.

\subsection{Generic convergence infrastructure}
\nolinkurl{LegendreSeriesConvergence.lean} develops a theorem for smooth functions whose repeated derivatives satisfy suitable endpoint or norm bounds.  Integration by parts transfers derivatives from the function to the Legendre eigenstructure, yielding decay of coefficients faster than any fixed power of \(n\).

For the Fabius up-function, compact support and smoothness supply these hypotheses to every order.  Consequently the coefficient sequence is absolutely summable.  Since \(|P_n(x)|\leq1\), the Weierstrass M-test gives uniform convergence on \([-1,1]\).

The generic theorem is separated from Fabius-specific coefficient arithmetic.  This permits future reuse for other compactly supported smooth functions and keeps the main convergence proof independent of exact moment formulas.

\subsection{Identifying the uniform limit}
Orthogonality first shows that the partial sums are the \(L^2\) projections of \(\RvachevUp\).  Absolute/uniform convergence produces a continuous limit \(S\).  To prove \(S=\RvachevUp\), the source may use one of two equivalent routes:
\begin{itemize}
  \item completeness of Legendre polynomials in \(L^2[-1,1]\), followed by equality of continuous representatives; or
  \item a generic convergence theorem already identifying the pointwise limit for sufficiently smooth functions.
\end{itemize}
The final theorem states a \nolinkurl{HasSum} at each point, then upgrades to uniform convergence of the function series.

Endpoints are included.  This matters because pointwise convergence theorems for orthogonal series often state only interior convergence.  The absolute coefficient bound and \(|P_n(\pm1)|=1\) handle endpoints directly.

\subsection{Translated series for the global Fabius function}
\nolinkurl{FabiusTranslatedLegendreSeries.lean} transports the up-function expansion to an interval such as \([0,2]\) where a translate agrees with the signed global Fabius function.  The transformation replaces \(P_{2n}(x)\) by \(P_{2n}(x-1)\) or the corresponding affine normalization.

The module also expands translated Legendre polynomials into ordinary monomials.  This produces an explicit power-polynomial series in the translated coordinate, with exact rational coefficients.  Uniform convergence follows from the original series under composition with the affine map.

\subsection{Least-squares optimality}
\nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreLeastSquares.lean} proves that the partial Legendre sum is the unique least-squares approximant among polynomials of the corresponding degree.  Evenness gives a useful strengthening: the visible even sum through \(P_{2N}\) is the full orthogonal projection through degree \(2N+1\), because the next odd coefficient vanishes.  It is therefore uniquely least-squares optimal among \emph{all} real polynomials of degree at most \(2N+1\), one degree beyond its own visible degree.  Let
\[
  S_N^{\mathrm{even}}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}a_{2n}P_{2n}.
\]
For any polynomial \(p\) with \(\deg p\leq2N+1\),
\[
  \lVert\RvachevUp-p\rVert_2^2
  =\lVert\RvachevUp-S_N^{\mathrm{even}}\rVert_2^2
   +\lVert S_N^{\mathrm{even}}-p\rVert_2^2.
\]
This Pythagorean identity follows because
\(\RvachevUp-S_N^{\mathrm{even}}\) is orthogonal to every polynomial in that
degree class.

The formal proof represents the finite span either as a submodule of \(L^2\)-type functions or directly through polynomial coefficients.  It proves orthogonality term by term and then expands the square of the norm.  Uniqueness follows because equality of errors forces
\(\lVert S_N^{\mathrm{even}}-p\rVert_2=0\), hence equality almost everywhere;
polynomial continuity upgrades this to pointwise polynomial equality.

\subsection{Central cancellation in the finite even-mode synthesis}
The existing Legendre translate synthesis has the exact mesh \(M=4^n\) for
the even mode.  Evaluating it at zero gives the representation-frontier
corollary
\[
 Q_{2n}(0)+2\sum_{0<k<M}Q_{2n}(k/M)\RvachevUp(k/M)
 =(-1)^n\binom{2n}{n}.
\]
\nolinkurl{RvachevLegendreCentralSum.lean} has no public definitions and
exactly three public theorems:
\nolinkurl{eval_legendrePolynomial_even_zero},
\nolinkurl{eval_rvachevLegendreDeconvolutionPolynomial_even}, and
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreCentralSum}.  The last quantifies over every
\nolinkurl{F : BoundedFabius} satisfying \nolinkurl{IsFabius F} and includes
\(n=0\).

The proof starts from the finite synthesis block \(-2M<k<2M\).  Compact
support removes the terms with \(M\leq |k|<2M\).  The second theorem makes the
deconvolved even mode even; evenness of \(\RvachevUp\) then pairs \(k\) with
\(-k\), and the central term uses \(\RvachevUp(0)=1\).  Clearing \(M=4^n\)
and applying the first theorem,
\(P_{2n}(0)=(-1)^n4^{-n}\binom{2n}{n}\), proves the formula.  Thus
\nolinkurl{cor:leg-central-sum} is \emph{Exact/Complete}.  This result does not
add a Jacobi closed form, all-degree decoder parity or rationality, reverse
spectral closure, mesh minimality, or a larger Lagrange right inverse.

\subsection{Finite Legendre--Rvachev biorthogonality}
The subsequent exhaustive one-definition/one-theorem leaf
\nolinkurl{RvachevLegendreBiorthogonality.lean} defines
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreAnalysisKernel} by
\[
 \Lambda_m(c)=\frac{2m+1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}
 \RvachevUp(x-c)P_m(x)\Differential x
\]
and proves \nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreBiorthogonality}.  Thus
\nolinkurl{def:leg-Lambda} is Exact.  For every
\nolinkurl{F : BoundedFabius} satisfying \nolinkurl{IsFabius F}, natural
\(M\ne0\), arbitrary \(\ell,m\), and
\(\ell\leq\TwoAdicValuation(M)\), its literal open-block identity is
\[
 \frac1M\sum_{-2M<k<2M}Q_\ell^-(k/M)\Lambda_m(k/M)=\delta_{m\ell}.
\]
There is no restriction on the analysis degree \(m\).  The proof inserts the
degree-\(\ell\) finite synthesis on \([-1,1]\), commutes its finite sum with
the normalized Legendre integral, and applies orthogonality.  Hence
\nolinkurl{thm:leg-biorthogonality} is \emph{Exact/Complete}, including
degree zero.  The larger \nolinkurl{thm:leg-Lambda} support, smoothness,
parity, origin-value, and Fourier--Bessel claims remain Partial, and
\nolinkurl{cor:leg-biorthogonal-matrices} remains Partial: the reverse
projector, its rank and trace, and Cauchy--Binet are not formalized, and the
leaf proves no dyadic rationality theorem for \(\Lambda_m\).

The later module \nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreEnergy.lean} closes the coefficient
energy side of the same expansion.  It defines the polynomial-form blocks
\(B_n=u_nP_{2n}\) and proves their complete orthogonality, including the
diagonal value \(2u_n^2/(4n+1)\).  It then proves Parseval for \(\RvachevUp\), the
exact shifted coefficient tail for every partial-sum squared error in both
\nolinkurl{HasSum} and \nolinkurl{tsum} form, and
\[
  \int_0^1 F(t)^2\,dt
  =\sum_{n\geq0}\frac{u_n^2}{4n+1}.
\]
The block is defined directly as a polynomial.  These declarations do not
formalize its finite up-translate realization, the separate Fourier-product
or infinite-sinc integral for this energy, or rationality of its partial sums.

\subsection{Executable Gaunt coefficients and finite up-law Gram sums}
\label{ch:legendre-gaunt}

The post-snapshot module \nolinkurl{LegendreGaunt.lean} closes the finite
triple-product calculation over the rationals.  Its exhaustive public API consists of
four definitions,
\nolinkurl{legendreLebesgueMomentRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt}, and
\nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat}, and twelve theorems,
\nolinkurl{legendreLebesgueMomentRat_even},
\nolinkurl{legendreLebesgueMomentRat_odd},
\nolinkurl{legendreLebesgueMomentRat_cast},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_momentFunctional},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_cast},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_swap_left},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_swap_right},
\nolinkurl{legendrePolynomial_mul_eq_sum_gaunt},
\nolinkurl{legendrePolynomialRat_mul_eq_sum_gaunt},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_of_odd_sum},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_of_add_lt}, and
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_of_triangle_violation}.
Writing \(\mu_n^{\mathrm L}\) for
\nolinkurl{legendreLebesgueMomentRat}\((n)\), the definition and its first three
theorems say exactly
\[
 \mu_n^{\mathrm L}=
 \begin{cases}
   2/(n+1),&n\ \text{even},\\
   0,&n\ \text{odd},
 \end{cases}
 \qquad
 (\mu_n^{\mathrm L}:\RealNumbers)=\int_{-1}^{1}x^n\,\Differential x.
\]
Thus \(\mu_{2n}^{\mathrm L}=2/(2n+1)\) and
\(\mu_{2n+1}^{\mathrm L}=0\).  If \(p_{n,a}=[X^a]P_n\) denotes the executable
rational Legendre coefficient, the rational Gaunt definition is the bounded sum
\[
 G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}
 =\sum_{a=0}^{i}\sum_{b=0}^{j}\sum_{c=0}^{k}
   p_{i,a}p_{j,b}p_{k,c}\,\mu_{a+b+c}^{\mathrm L},
\]
while \nolinkurl{legendreGaunt}\((i,j,k)\) is
\[
 G_{ijk}=\int_{-1}^{1}P_i(x)P_j(x)P_k(x)\,\Differential x,
 \qquad
 L_{ijk}=\frac{2k+1}{2}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}
\]
is \nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat}\((i,j,k)\).
The moment-functional theorem identifies the bounded sum algebraically, and the cast
theorem gives \((G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}:\RealNumbers)=G_{ijk}\).  The two swap
theorems exchange the first pair or the last pair of indices.  The real and rational
product theorems then give, without extra hypotheses,
\[
 P_iP_j=\sum_{k=0}^{i+j}L_{ijk}P_k
\]
in \(\RealNumbers[X]\) and \(\RationalNumbers[X]\), respectively.

All four definitions are total at every \(i,j,k\in\NaturalNumbers\).  The only
hypotheses in the public theorem surface occur in the three support results:
\(\operatorname{Odd}(i+j+k)\) implies \(G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}=0\);
\(i+j<k\) implies the same; and any strict triangle violation
\[
 i+j<k\ \lor\ i+k<j\ \lor\ j+k<i
\]
implies the same.  These are necessary support zeros only.  There is no converse
support theorem, zero-row Wigner-square identification, factorial closed form,
positivity theorem, or asymptotic assertion in \nolinkurl{LegendreGaunt.lean}
itself.  The next focused leaf closes the first four gaps for the total squared
zero-row integer-index datum and sharpens support to an equivalence.

The exhaustive public API of \nolinkurl{LegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} consists
of exactly two definitions, \nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible} and
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and exactly twenty-five theorems:
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible_iff_exists_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pairwise_add_factorial},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_factorial_of_halfSum},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_zero_of_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_add_boundary},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_add_boundary_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_zero_left},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_zero_left_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_pairwise_add_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_of_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat_eq_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and
\nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat_pos_iff_admissible}.

Admissibility means even total degree and the three weak triangle inequalities; it is
equivalent to
\[
 (i,j,k)=(b+c,a+c,a+b)
 \qquad(a,b,c\in\NaturalNumbers).
\]
The total rational datum \(W_{ijk}^{2,\RationalNumbers}\) is zero outside this support.
In pairwise-sum coordinates it is
\[
 W_{b+c,a+c,a+b}^{2,\RationalNumbers}
 =\frac{\binom{2a}{a}\binom{2b}{b}\binom{2c}{c}}
 {(2(a+b+c)+1)\binom{2(a+b+c)}{a+b+c}},
\]
with both pairwise-coordinate and half-sum factorial forms.  The half-sum theorem assumes
exactly \(i+j+k=2s\) and \(i,j,k\leq s\).  The global identities are
\[
 G_{ijk}^{\RationalNumbers}=2W_{ijk}^{2,\RationalNumbers},
 \qquad
 G_{ijk}=2\bigl(W_{ijk}^{2,\RationalNumbers}:\RealNumbers\bigr),
 \qquad
 L_{ijk}=(2k+1)W_{ijk}^{2,\RationalNumbers}.
\]
The square datum and both Gaunt coefficients are positive exactly on the admissible
support, vanish exactly off it, and are nonnegative everywhere; \(L_{ijk}\) has the
same sharp positivity support.  Separate central-binomial boundary theorems cover
\(k=i+j\) and \(i=0\).  This total object is only the \emph{squared zero-row
integer-index} datum: there is no signed symbol or phase convention, half-integer or
nonzero-magnetic-index theory, general \(3j/6j/9j\) symbol, Wigner orthogonality, or
recoupling identity.

The companion \nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreGaunt.lean} converts those product
linearizations into finite formulas for the Rvachev Legendre Gram entries.  Its
exhaustive public API has one definition,
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat}, and eight theorems,
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat_even},
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat_odd},
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat_cast},
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat_eq_normalized_moment},
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat_eq_sum_full_gaunt},
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat_eq_sum_gaunt},
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramMatrixRat_apply_eq_sum_gaunt}, and
\nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix_apply_eq_sum_gaunt}.
If \(c_r^{\RationalNumbers}\) is
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat}\((r)\), its full extension is
\[
 \FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{k}=
 \begin{cases}
   c_{k/2}^{\RationalNumbers},&2\mid k,\\
   0,&2\nmid k.
 \end{cases}
\]
The even and odd theorems give
\(\FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{2n}=c_n^{\RationalNumbers}\) and
\(\FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{2n+1}=0\).  Unconditionally in \(k\), the
normalized-moment theorem says that \(\FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{k}\) is
\((2k+1)/2\) times \nolinkurl{momentFunctional} with moment sequence
\nolinkurl{rvachevRawMomentRat}, evaluated at
\nolinkurl{legendrePolynomialRat}\((k)\).  In abbreviated notation,
\[
 \FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{k}
 =\frac{2k+1}{2}\,\mathcal M_{\mathrm{Rv}}(P_k^{\RationalNumbers}).
\]
For arbitrary natural \(i,j\), write \(R_{ij}^{\RationalNumbers}\) for
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat}\((i,j)\).  The two rational entry
theorems state exactly
\[
 R_{ij}^{\RationalNumbers}
 =\sum_{k=0}^{i+j}\FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{k}
    G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}
 =\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}c_r^{\RationalNumbers}
    G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ij,2r}.
\]
The rational matrix theorem supplies the second equality entrywise for every
\(i,j\in\operatorname{Fin} n\).

Exactly two public theorems in this companion require analytic hypotheses.
Given \(F\) of type \nolinkurl{BoundedFabius} and a proof \(h_F\) of
\nolinkurl{IsFabius F},
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat_cast} identifies the real cast
of \(\FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{k}\) with
\nolinkurl{rvachevFullLegendreCoefficient}\((F,k)\), and
\nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix_apply_eq_sum_gaunt} states, writing
\(D^F_{n,ij}\) for \nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix}\((F,n,i,j)\),
\[
 D^F_{n,ij}
 =\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}c_r(F)\,G_{ij,2r},
\]
where \(c_r(F)\) is \nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreCoefficient}\((F,r)\), for
\(i,j\in\operatorname{Fin} n\).  The other six theorems are unconditional finite
identities.  No infinite Legendre series is interchanged with integration, and this
module itself proves no Wigner-square or factorial formula, Christoffel
reconstruction, or new positivity or asymptotic result.  The preceding
\nolinkurl{LegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} supplies the squared integer zero-row datum,
factorial forms, sharp support, and positivity independently.  The final finite
composition leaf makes that substitution; neither companion chooses a signed phase or
treats half-integer or nonzero magnetic indices, orthogonality, or recoupling.

\nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} has no public definitions and exactly
three public theorems:
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramMatrixRat_apply_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and
\nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix_apply_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}.
For arbitrary natural \(i,j\), the first is the unconditional rational identity
\[
 R_{ij}^{\RationalNumbers}
 =2\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}
 c_r^{\RationalNumbers}W_{ij,2r}^{2,\RationalNumbers}.
\]
For \(i,j\in\operatorname{Fin} n\), the executable rational matrix theorem is
\[
 R_{n,ij}^{\RationalNumbers}
 =2\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}
 c_r^{\RationalNumbers}W_{ij,2r}^{2,\RationalNumbers}.
\]
The third theorem assumes exactly \(F\) of type \nolinkurl{BoundedFabius} and
\(h_F\) of type \nolinkurl{IsFabius F}; for \(i,j\in\operatorname{Fin} n\) it states
\[
 D^F_{n,ij}
 =2\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}
 c_r(F)\bigl(W_{ij,2r}^{2,\RationalNumbers}:\RealNumbers\bigr).
\]
Thus the exact boundary is finite rational entry, rational matrix, and real up-law
matrix sums against the total squared zero-row integer datum.  The construction is a
finite substitution only: it chooses no signed \(3j\) phase and adds no half-integer,
nonzero-magnetic-index, general Wigner-symbol, orthogonality, recoupling, or
infinite-series result.

\subsubsection{Expanded zero-row Wigner-square formula and proof ledger}
\label{ch:legendre-gaunt-closed-form}

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Source/PDF parity convention.}\space
This source contains the exhaustive two-definition, twenty-eight-theorem closed-form
inventory and the expanded formula-and-proof ledger in this subsubsection.  The
external repository metadata certifies only its named historical source/PDF
checkpoint; this merged source postdates that receipt, and a fresh uninterrupted
three-pass Libertinus parity rebuild remains pending.  Repository checksum ledgers
have been abolished.
\end{boxedremark}

The limitations just stated remain exact descriptions of the two predecessor modules.
The downstream core \nolinkurl{LegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} closes precisely the
integer-index, zero-row \emph{squared} problem.  It has exactly two public definitions:
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible} and
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat}.  Write
\[
 \mathsf A(i,j,k)\Longleftrightarrow
 (i+j+k)\bmod 2=0\ \land\
 i\le j+k\ \land\ j\le i+k\ \land\ k\le i+j,
\]
put \(C_n=\binom{2n}{n}\), and let \(s=\Floor{(i+j+k)/2}\).  The second definition
is the total rational datum
\[
 W^2_{ijk}=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\frac{C_{s-i}C_{s-j}C_{s-k}}{(2s+1)C_s},
     &\mathsf A(i,j,k),\\[0.8em]
 0,&\neg\mathsf A(i,j,k).
 \end{cases}
\]
The notation \(W^2\) is expository: Lean defines this square datum directly, not as the
square of a separately defined signed symbol.

Its exhaustive public API then has exactly twenty-five theorems, in source order:
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible_iff_exists_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pairwise_add_factorial},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_factorial_of_halfSum},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_zero_of_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_add_boundary},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_add_boundary_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_zero_left},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_zero_left_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_pairwise_add_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_of_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat_eq_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and
\nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat_pos_iff_admissible}.

The first structural theorem gives the exact triangle-coordinate parametrization
\[
 \mathsf A(i,j,k)\Longleftrightarrow
 \exists a,b,c\in\NaturalNumbers,\qquad
 i=b+c,\quad j=a+c,\quad k=a+b.
\]
If \(S=a+b+c\), the two closed forms on this support are
\[
 W^2_{b+c,a+c,a+b}
 =\frac{C_aC_bC_c}{(2S+1)C_S}
 =\frac{(S!)^2(2a)!(2b)!(2c)!}
 {(2S+1)!(a!)^2(b!)^2(c!)^2}.
\]
The separately named half-sum theorem says that, under the explicit hypotheses
\(i+j+k=2s\) and \(i,j,k\le s\),
\[
 W^2_{ijk}
 =\frac{(s!)^2(2(s-i))!(2(s-j))!(2(s-k))!}
 {(2s+1)!((s-i)!)^2((s-j)!)^2((s-k)!)^2}.
\]
Those dominance hypotheses matter because all indices and subtractions in the Lean
statement are natural-number operations.

For \(G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}=\text{\ttfamily legendreGauntRat}(i,j,k)\), the public
boundary formulas are
\[
 G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i,j,i+j}
 =\frac{2C_iC_j}{(2(i+j)+1)C_{i+j}}
 =2W^2_{i,j,i+j},
\qquad
 G^{\RationalNumbers}_{0,j,k}
 =\begin{cases}2/(2j+1),&j=k,\\0,&j\ne k,\end{cases}
 =2W^2_{0,j,k}.
\]
The identification is global, not merely a necessary support statement:
\[
 G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}=2W^2_{ijk},\qquad
 \text{\ttfamily legendreGaunt}(i,j,k)=2(W^2_{ijk}:\RealNumbers).
\]
For each of \(W^2_{ijk}\), \(G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}\), and the real Gaunt integral,
the API separately proves positivity exactly on \(\mathsf A\), zero exactly off
\(\mathsf A\), and global nonnegativity.  Finally,
\[
 \text{\ttfamily legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat}(i,j,k)
 =(2k+1)W^2_{ijk},
\]
and that coefficient is positive exactly on \(\mathsf A(i,j,k)\).

\begin{proofidea}
The proof stays inside the executable rational Legendre layer.  Let
\(\lambda_n=[X^n]P_n=2^{-n}C_n\).  In the finite product linearization of \(P_iP_j\),
only its \(P_{i+j}\) term reaches degree \(i+j\), so
\[
 \lambda_i\lambda_j
 =\frac{2(i+j)+1}{2}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i,j,i+j}\lambda_{i+j}.
\]
Solving this identity gives the degenerate-triangle boundary above.  The zero-left law
uses the diagonal boundary together with the already proved triangle zeros.

The private engine next derives the rational Bonnet recurrence
\[
 XP_n=\frac{n+1}{2n+1}P_{n+1}+\frac n{2n+1}P_{n-1}.
\]
Applying the rational moment functional to
\((XP_i)P_jP_k=P_i(XP_j)P_k\) gives
\[
 \frac{i+1}{2i+1}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i+1,j,k}
 +\frac{i}{2i+1}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i-1,j,k}
 =\frac{j+1}{2j+1}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i,j+1,k}
 +\frac{j}{2j+1}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i,j-1,k}.
\]
The proposed central-binomial quotient obeys the corresponding recurrence by
\((n+1)C_{n+1}=2(2n+1)C_n\).  A nested induction in the pairwise coordinates uses the
degenerate-triangle values on its two boundary faces, the recurrence in the interior,
and cancellation of a strictly positive rational leading coefficient.  Thus
\(G^{\RationalNumbers}=2W^2\) for all pairwise-sum triples.  The coordinate equivalence
covers the admissible support; the predecessor parity and strict-triangle zeros settle
its complement.  Replacing \(C_n\) by \((2n)!/(n!)^2\), and using positivity of central
binomials, yields the factorial, exact-support, and sign packages.  The real result is
then the existing cast theorem.  In particular, the proof imports no external Wigner
integral evaluation or representation-theory theorem.
\end{proofidea}

The wrapper \nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} has no public definition and
exactly three public theorems:
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramMatrixRat_apply_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and
\nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix_apply_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}.
The first two are unconditional rational identities:
\[
 R^{\RationalNumbers}_{ij}
 =2\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}
 c_r^{\RationalNumbers}W^2_{i,j,2r},
\]
first for \nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat} and then entrywise for
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramMatrixRat}.  The third requires
\(F:\text{\ttfamily BoundedFabius}\) and \(h_F:\text{\ttfamily IsFabius}\ F\), and for
\(i,j:\operatorname{Fin}n\) states
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 &\text{\ttfamily upLegendreGramMatrix}(F,n,i,j)\\
 &\quad=2\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{((i:\NaturalNumbers)+(j:\NaturalNumbers))/2}}
   \text{\ttfamily rvachevLegendreCoefficient}(F,r)\\
 &\qquad{}\times
   \left(W^2_{(i:\NaturalNumbers),(j:\NaturalNumbers),2r}:\RealNumbers\right).
 \end{aligned}
\]
All three sums are finite, with Lean's actual index set
\(\text{\ttfamily range}(\Floor{(i+j)/2}+1)\).  Their proofs simply substitute the global
Gaunt identity into the three predecessor sums and move the scalar \(2\) outside.

The boundary of this API is deliberate and exact.  It defines only a total rational
square datum for natural angular indices and a zero magnetic row.  There is no signed
symbol and no Condon--Shortley or other phase convention; in particular, there is no Lean
theorem whose left side is the square of a formal signed Wigner term.  There are no
half-integer or nonzero-magnetic-index symbols, no general \(3j\), \(6j\), or \(9j\)
theory, and no Wigner orthogonality or recoupling result.  The wrapper is a finite-sum
transport only and asserts no infinite Legendre-series interchange.

\subsection{Computational significance}
The exact coefficient theorem gives a certified way to produce rational polynomial approximants.  The least-squares theorem certifies optimality in \(L^2\), while uniform convergence certifies pointwise approximation.  This is a rare combination:
\begin{itemize}
  \item exact arithmetic coefficients;
  \item kernel-checked analytic convergence; and
  \item an optimality theorem for every finite truncation.
\end{itemize}
The evaluator and moment recurrences can be used to compute coefficients without numerical integration.

\section{The k-fold Thue--Morse draft audit}\label{ch:kfold}

\subsection{Purpose of the audit}
\nolinkurl{PaperKFoldThueMorse.lean} collects a separate line of work concerning k-fold Thue--Morse constructions and a related informal draft.  Its role is both constructive and diagnostic: it proves exact identities and corrected approximation statements, but it also formalizes counterexamples to several literal claims in the draft.

This part of the repository illustrates a mature use of formalization.  A failed proof is not treated as an obstacle to be papered over; the surrounding hypotheses are tested, false versions are refuted, and repaired versions are isolated.

\subsection{Exact finite identities}
The Thue--Morse modules prove prefix-sum cancellation, long zero runs of finite differences, convolution formulas, and generating-series identities.  Repeated finite difference annihilates polynomials below a threshold degree, which explains the high-order cancellation of the sign sequence.

The full row-level symmetry is sharper than the endpoint cancellation.  If
$k,r,d\in\NaturalNumbers$, $k\le r$, and $k+d<2^r$, then
\[
 \sigma_k(2^r-k-1-d)=(-1)^{r-k}\sigma_k(d),
 \qquad
 \sigma_k(2^r-k-1-d)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \sigma_k(d)=0.
\]
These are exactly
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_reverse_window} and
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_reverse_window_eq_zero_iff} in
\nolinkurl{ThueMorsePrefix.lean}.  At $k=0$, the identity is the binary
complement theorem \nolinkurl{thueMorseSign_dyadic_complement} from
\nolinkurl{DyadicClosedForm.lean}; at $d=0$, the statement specializes, using
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_at_zero}, namely $\sigma_k(0)=1$, to the sharp left
boundary \nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_before_dyadic_run} of the final dyadic zero run.  The
terminal zero run and the value $-1$ at its right boundary are separate
theorems, exposed by \nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_endpoint},
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_zero_reverse},
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_zero_run}, and
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_at_dyadic}.  In particular, the reflected-zero
equivalence pairs zeros in the pre-run window but does not assert that the
window is zero-free.

The proof mirrors the finite-difference geometry.  Dyadic bit complementation
settles order zero, the left boundary seeds offset zero, and a nested induction
uses
$\sigma_{k+1}(n+1)-\sigma_{k+1}(n)=\sigma_k(n+1)$ to propagate the same signed
reflection in both prefix order and offset.  Since the sign is always
$\pm1$, the zero-locus equivalence follows without any cancellation
hypothesis.

The formal proofs use induction on binary depth and repeated even/odd splitting.  Convolution associativity is kept finite, so all sums are over \nolinkurl{Finset.range} and can be reindexed explicitly.  Generating polynomials turn convolution into multiplication, allowing coefficient comparison to replace nested sum manipulations.

\nolinkurl{ThueMorseBinomialLog.lean} also makes the zero--one convention
exact.  For the integer sum
\(X(n)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{\binom nk}\), it proves
\(n+1-X(n)=2^{\operatorname{wt}(n)+1}\), so the ensuing base-two logarithm
is exact.  If \(t_n=\operatorname{wt}(n)\bmod2\), the resulting rational
identity is
\[
 t_n=\frac{1+(-1)^{\log_2(n+1-X(n))}}2.
\]
The module also identifies, in every ring,
\((-1)^{t_n}\) with the cast of the signed Thue--Morse convention used by the
real and complex \(q\)-series.

\subsection{The failed polygon and the corrected grid samples}
The literal grid of the draft uses
\(S^k_j/2^{\binom{k}{2}}\) at \(j/2^k\).  At \(x=1\) its value is
\(-2^{-\binom{k}{2}}\), so neither the grid nor either intended polygonal
interpolation can converge there to a function with value one.  This is
formalized by \nolinkurl{paperPrefixGridValue_endpoint_not_tendsto_one},
\nolinkurl{paperPrefixPolygon_endpoint_not_tendsto_one}, and
\nolinkurl{paperPrefixPolygonReal_endpoint_not_tendsto_one} in
\nolinkurl{ThueMorseGenerating.lean}.

The repaired theorem uses the shifted, dyadic-floor samples
\nolinkurl{correctedPrefixGridSample}, not a continuous polygon.  Away from the
right endpoint each sample is exactly \nolinkurl{stepApproximant n} at
\nolinkurl{correctedPrefixCellCenter n x}; those centers tend to \(2x-1\).  The
endpoint is handled separately, giving pointwise convergence on \([0,1]\) to
\(\RvachevUp(2x-1)\), and sampling at \(x/2\) recovers \(F(x)\).  No quantitative
uniform rate or continuity of the sample function is asserted.

\subsection{Formal counterexamples}
\nolinkurl{DraftCounterexamples.lean} gives exact witnesses for three distinct
failures.  At \(k=2,j=1\), the proposed slope is \(-2\), whereas
\(F'(1/4)=1\); the error is therefore \(3>4h=1\), contradicting the claimed
local bound.  For every \(x>0\), the proxy ratio from equation~(7) is
\[
  \frac{x\,2^{n+1}}{n+1}\longrightarrow+\infty,
\]
so the terms are unbounded and the displayed maximum does not exist.
Finally, under the draft's equation~(8), the Stirling proxy retains an
additive \(+n\) term; equation~(10) cannot absorb it into \(O(\log n)\).

These are arithmetic or elementary asymptotic counterexamples, not
subsequence arguments imported from the coarse Fabius endpoint theorem.  Each
negative result is stated independently of the prose whose claim it refutes.

\subsection{Repairing the lower Lambert branch}
The draft uses a lower branch of a Lambert-type inverse.  The repository
develops both real branches with explicit domains, order, and inversion
theorems; no appeal is made to an ambiguous multivalued inverse.  The raw
endpoint continuity layer is now closed: in \nolinkurl{LowerLambertW.lean},
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_continuousWithinAt_branchPoint} and
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_continuousOn_Ico} give continuity of \(W_{-1}\) on
\([-\exp(-1),0)\), while \nolinkurl{PrincipalLambertW.lean} supplies
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_continuousWithinAt_branchPoint} and
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_continuousOn_Ici} on
\([-\exp(-1),\infty)\).  At the ordinary point zero,
\nolinkurl{deriv_principalLambertW_zero} states
\(\operatorname{deriv}W_0(0)=1\), and
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_isEquivalent_zero} packages \(W_0(z)\sim z\).
The later module \nolinkurl{LambertWCurvature.lean} closes the regular-domain
second-derivative and shape layer.  On either smooth branch it uses the
nonsingular exponential form
\[
 W''(z)=-\frac{e^{-2W(z)}(W(z)+2)}{(W(z)+1)^3}.
\]
For the principal branch, \nolinkurl{deriv_principalLambertW},
\nolinkurl{deriv_principalLambertW_hasDerivAt}, and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_principalLambertW} give the first derivative and the
derivative-of-derivative formula throughout
\((-e^{-1},\infty)\), including \(z=0\).  The simplification
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_principalLambertW_zero} gives \(W_0''(0)=-2\),
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_principalLambertW_neg} proves strict negativity on the
open domain, and \nolinkurl{strictConcaveOn_principalLambertW} extends strict
concavity to the closed natural domain \([-e^{-1},\infty)\).

For \(W_{-1}\), \nolinkurl{deriv_lowerLambertW_hasDerivAt} and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW} give the same formula on
\((-e^{-1},0)\).  The three exact sign declarations
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW_pos_iff},
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW_neg_iff}, and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW_eq_zero_iff} locate the unique change at
\(z=-2e^{-2}\): the second derivative is positive to its left, negative to
its right, and zero exactly there.  Consequently
\nolinkurl{strictConvexOn_lowerLambertW_left} proves strict convexity on
\([-e^{-1},-2e^{-2}]\), while
\nolinkurl{strictConcaveOn_lowerLambertW_right} proves strict concavity on
\([-2e^{-2},0)\).  These are regular-interior derivative results plus shape
theorems that include the finite branch point and shared inflection endpoint;
the lower domain remains open at zero.

\subsubsection{A global coordinate for the branch pair}
The two branches on their common open domain admit a more economical
description than two unrelated inverse functions.  For
\(-\exp(-1)<x<0\), define
\[
 a=W_0(x),\qquad b=W_{-1}(x),\qquad
 \delta=a-b>0.
\]
The exact forward identities are
\begin{align*}
 a&=-\frac{\delta}{\exp(\delta)-1},&
 b&=-\frac{\delta\exp(\delta)}{\exp(\delta)-1}
    =-\frac{\delta}{1-\exp(-\delta)},\\
 x&=-\frac{\delta}{\exp(\delta)-1}
       \exp\!\left(-\frac{\delta}{\exp(\delta)-1}\right).
\end{align*}
They come from one cancellation.  Since
\(a\exp(a)=b\exp(b)=x\) and \(a,b\ne0\), division gives
\(b/a=\exp(a-b)=\exp(\delta)\).  Thus
\(b=a\exp(\delta)\) and
\(\delta=a-b=a(1-\exp(\delta))\), which yields the first two formulas;
the defining equation yields the third.

The converse is just as explicit.  For \(\delta>0\), define
\[
 A(\delta)=-\frac{\delta}{\exp(\delta)-1},\quad
 B(\delta)=-\frac{\delta\exp(\delta)}{\exp(\delta)-1},\quad
 X(\delta)=A(\delta)\exp(A(\delta)).
\]
The strict exponential tangent inequalities give
\(-1<A(\delta)<0\) and \(B(\delta)<-1\).  Direct algebra gives
\(B=A-\delta\) and \(B\exp(B)=A\exp(A)\), so branch uniqueness proves
\(W_0(X(\delta))=A(\delta)\),
\(W_{-1}(X(\delta))=B(\delta)\), and
\(W_0(X(\delta))-W_{-1}(X(\delta))=\delta\).  Conversely,
substituting the gap of \(x\) into \(X\) returns \(x\).  Hence the branch-gap
map is a bijection
\[
 (-\exp(-1),0)\longleftrightarrow(0,\infty).
\]
With \(t=\exp(\delta)>1\), the inverse coordinate becomes
\[
 W_0=-\frac{\log t}{t-1},\qquad
 W_{-1}=-\frac{t\log t}{t-1},\qquad
 x=-\frac{\log t}{t-1}\,t^{-1/(t-1)}.
\]

Adding, multiplying, and dividing the paired formulas gives
\[
 \frac{W_{-1}(x)}{W_0(x)}=\exp(\delta),\qquad
 W_0(x)+W_{-1}(x)=-\delta\coth(\delta/2),\qquad
 W_0(x)W_{-1}(x)=\frac{\delta^2}{4\sinh^2(\delta/2)}.
\]
For \(y>0\), the inequalities \(\sinh y>y\) and
\(y\coth y>1\) prove the strict consequences
\[
 W_0(x)+W_{-1}(x)<-2,
 \qquad 0<W_0(x)W_{-1}(x)<1.
\]

The Lean layering follows the proof rather than merging all of this into one
opaque theorem.  \nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPairing.lean} has no definitions
and exactly seven theorems:
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_sub_lowerLambertW_pos},
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_eq_principalLambertW_mul_exp_gap},
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_eq_neg_gap_div},
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_eq_neg_gap_mul_exp_div},
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_eq_neg_gap_div_one_sub_exp_neg},
\nolinkurl{eq_neg_gap_div_mul_exp}, and
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_lowerLambertW_eq_of_exp_gap}.
\nolinkurl{LambertWGapBijection.lean} has the four definitions
\nolinkurl{gapPrincipal}, \nolinkurl{gapLower}, \nolinkurl{gapArg}, and
\nolinkurl{branchGap}, followed by exactly sixteen theorems:
\nolinkurl{gap_denominator_pos}, \nolinkurl{gapPrincipal_mem_Ioo},
\nolinkurl{gapLower_eq_mul_exp}, \nolinkurl{gapLower_eq_sub},
\nolinkurl{gapLower_lt_neg_one}, \nolinkurl{gapLower_mul_exp},
\nolinkurl{gapArg_mem_Ioo}, \nolinkurl{principalLambertW_gapArg},
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_gapArg}, \nolinkurl{branchGap_gapArg},
\nolinkurl{gapArg_branchGap}, \nolinkurl{branchGap_invOn},
\nolinkurl{branchGap_bijOn}, \nolinkurl{principalLambertW_gapArg_log},
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_gapArg_log}, and \nolinkurl{gapArg_log}.
The zero-definition/nine-theorem
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchSymmetry.lean} surface is
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_div_principalLambertW_eq_exp_branchGap},
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_add_lowerLambertW_eq_exp_branchGap},
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_add_lowerLambertW_eq_cosh_div_sinh_branchGap},
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_mul_lowerLambertW_eq_exp_branchGap},
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_mul_lowerLambertW_eq_sinh_sq_branchGap},
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_add_lowerLambertW_lt_neg_two},
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_mul_lowerLambertW_pos},
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_mul_lowerLambertW_lt_one}, and
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_mul_lowerLambertW_mem_Ioo}.

The openness of both intervals is substantive.  At the finite branch point
the gap is zero, the rational formulas have a zero denominator, and their
limiting sum and product are \(-2\) and \(1\), so the strict inequalities do
not extend there.  At zero the classical lower branch is singular; the
theorems make no assertion about its all-real totalization.

The separate leaf
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchGapBernoulli.lean} has no definitions and exactly
five theorems:
\nolinkurl{summable_norm_bernoulli_mul_pow_div_factorial},
\nolinkurl{summable_bernoulli_mul_pow_div_factorial_iff},
\nolinkurl{hasSum_bernoulli_mul_pow_div_factorial},
\nolinkurl{hasSum_bernoulli_mul_pow_div_factorial_complex_iff}, and
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_lowerLambertW_eq_bernoulliSeries}.
They prove absolute summability of
\(\sum_{n\geq0}B_nz^n/n!\) for real \(z\) with \(\lvert z\rvert<2\pi\), its
exact complex convergence locus
\[
 \sum_{n\geq0}B_nw^n/n!\text{ is summable}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \lvert w\rvert<2\pi,
\]
its actual real \nolinkurl{HasSum} value \(z/(\exp z-1)\) when \(z\ne0\),
its actual complex \nolinkurl{HasSum} value
\nolinkurl{complexExpm1Div}\((w)^{-1}\) throughout that disk, and, on
\(-e^{-1}<x<0\) with \(0<\delta<2\pi\),
\[
 W_0(x)=-\sum_{n\geq0}B_n\frac{\delta^n}{n!},\qquad
 W_{-1}(x)=-\delta-\sum_{n\geq0}B_n\frac{\delta^n}{n!}.
\]
Together with the preceding three finite branch-coordinate modules, this is
an exhaustive four-module surface of four definitions and thirty-seven
theorems, forty-one public declarations in all.

The analytic proof is explicit.  Put
\[
 q=\frac{\lvert z\rvert}{2\pi}<1,\qquad
 c_r=\frac{B_{2(r+1)}}{2(r+1)(2(r+1))!}.
\]
The even-zeta majorant from \nolinkurl{BasicBernoulliLog.lean} gives
\[
 \lvert c_rz^{2(r+1)}\rvert
 \le\frac{\pi^2}{6}q^{2(r+1)}.
\]
The odd Bernoulli numbers beyond \(B_1\) vanish, and an even EGF term is
\(2(r+1)c_rz^{2(r+1)}\).  Hence, for every \(n\ge2\),
\[
 \left\lvert\frac{B_nz^n}{n!}\right\rvert
 \le n\frac{\pi^2}{6}q^n.
\]
Comparison with the convergent series \(\sum n q^n\), plus the two initial
terms, proves absolute summability.

For complex \(w\), sufficiency follows from the same comparison because each
term norm depends only on \(\lvert w\rvert\).  For necessity, put
\[
 Z_{r+1}=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}m^{-2(r+1)}\ge1.
\]
The exact even-Bernoulli identity used by the Lean proof is
\[
 \left\lvert
   \frac{B_{2(r+1)}w^{2(r+1)}}{(2(r+1))!}
 \right\rvert
 =2Z_{r+1}
  \left(\frac{\lvert w\rvert}{2\pi}\right)^{2(r+1)}.
\]
When \(\lvert w\rvert\ge2\pi\), every displayed even-index term norm is at
least \(2\), so the terms fail to tend to zero.  Hence the complex series is
summable exactly inside the disk and diverges at every boundary or exterior
point.

For evaluation set
\[
 f_n=\frac{B_nz^n}{n!},\qquad
 g_n=\frac{z^n}{n!}-\IndicatorOf{\{n=0\}}.
\]
Both sequences are absolutely summable: this was just proved for \(f\), while
for \(g\) it follows from absolute summability of the exponential series and
the finite-support correction.  Its sum is \(\exp z-1\).  The formal identity
\[
 \left(\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{B_n}{n!}X^n\right)(\exp X-1)=X
\]
says, after multiplying the coefficient of degree \(n\) by \(z^n\), that
\[
 \sum_{i+j=n}f_i g_j=
 \begin{cases}z,&n=1,\\0,&n\ne1.\end{cases}
\]
The absolutely convergent Cauchy-product theorem therefore yields, over
\(\ComplexNumbers\),
\[
 \left(\sum_{n\ge0}f_n\right)(\exp z-1)=z.
\]
If \(z\ne0\), this identity itself forces \(\exp z-1\ne0\), and division
proves the complex quotient evaluation.  At \(z=0\), the series reduces to
its constant term \(B_0=1\).  The definition
\nolinkurl{complexExpm1Div} records exactly these two cases: it is
\((\exp z-1)/z\) away from zero and \(1\) at zero, so its inverse is the
canonical removable value of the Bernoulli EGF.  The theorem
\nolinkurl{hasSum_bernoulli_mul_pow_div_factorial_complex_iff} packages this
actual value together with the exact open-disk criterion.  For the branch
wrapper, positivity of \(\delta\) turns \(\delta<2\pi\) into
\(\lvert\delta\rvert<2\pi\); substituting the EGF in
\(W_0=-\delta/(\exp\delta-1)\) and then using \(W_{-1}=W_0-\delta\) proves the
paired series.

The last theorem is the exact Lean crosswalk for the compact
\nolinkurl{eq:pair-Bernoulli-general} in the retained Lambert guide, and the
complex \nolinkurl{HasSum} equivalence makes the radius-\(2\pi\), boundary
divergence, and canonical-removable reading of
\nolinkurl{eq:bernoulli-gen} Exact.  This does not assert the false literal
totalized quotient at the origin---where Lean evaluates
\(0/(\exp 0-1)\) as zero---or holomorphy of a named sum function.  No other
label in the surrounding corollary is promoted, and higher or convergent
Puiseux/logarithmic expansions remain open.

The next two raw-branch leaves resolve the formerly open branch-point
geometry.  Write
\[
 b=-\exp(-1),\qquad \Delta(z)=z-b=z+\exp(-1),\qquad
 \mathcal S(z)=\sqrt{2\exp(1)\bigl(z+\exp(-1)\bigr)}.
\]
All limits here use the right-hand filter \(z\downarrow b\).  The compiled
statements are
\[
 \operatorname{deriv}W_0(z)\longrightarrow+\infty,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{deriv}W_{-1}(z)\longrightarrow-\infty,
\]
and, for the secants drawn from the common endpoint \((b,-1)\),
\[
 \frac{W_0(z)+1}{z+\exp(-1)}\longrightarrow+\infty,
 \qquad
 \frac{W_{-1}(z)+1}{z+\exp(-1)}\longrightarrow-\infty.
\]
In particular neither branch has a finite right derivative: both fail

\begin{center}
\(\operatorname{DifferentiableWithinAt}_{\RealNumbers}
  (W_j,\operatorname{Ioi}(b),b)\).
\end{center}

The two totalized all-real branch functions also fail

\begin{center}
\(\operatorname{DifferentiableAt}_{\RealNumbers}(W_j,b)\).
\end{center}

The human proof follows the source layering.  Endpoint continuity gives
\(W_j(z)\to-1\), and the inverse derivative is
\[
 \frac{1}{\exp(W_j(z))(W_j(z)+1)}.
\]
Its denominator tends to zero from above on the principal branch and from
below on the lower branch, giving the two signed infinite derivative limits.
Principal concavity bounds its endpoint secant from below by the moving
derivative; lower convexity on the left shape interval bounds its endpoint
secant from above by the moving derivative.  Those inequalities force the
same signed limits for the secants.  A standard one-sided derivative-divergence
lemma rules out a finite within-set derivative, and ordinary differentiability
would imply that prohibited right-hand differentiability.

The complete public surface of
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointGeometry.lean} is exactly these eight theorems:
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.51\textwidth}
                         >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.43\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endhead
\nolinkurl{tendsto_deriv_principalLambertW_branchPoint_atTop} & Principal derivative tends to \(+\infty\) from the right. \\
\nolinkurl{tendsto_deriv_lowerLambertW_branchPoint_atBot} & Lower derivative tends to \(-\infty\) from the right. \\
\nolinkurl{tendsto_principalLambertW_secantSlope_branchPoint_atTop} & Principal endpoint secant slope tends to \(+\infty\). \\
\nolinkurl{tendsto_lowerLambertW_secantSlope_branchPoint_atBot} & Lower endpoint secant slope tends to \(-\infty\). \\
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_not_differentiableWithinAt_branchPoint} & No finite principal right derivative on \(\operatorname{Ioi}(b)\). \\
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_not_differentiableWithinAt_branchPoint} & No finite lower right derivative on \(\operatorname{Ioi}(b)\). \\
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_not_differentiableAt_branchPoint} & The totalized principal branch is not differentiable at \(b\). \\
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_not_differentiableAt_branchPoint} & The totalized lower branch is not differentiable at \(b\). \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

The asymptotic leaf proves the first square-root term.  For the forward map
\(\phi(w)=w\exp w\), one application of L'H\^opital's rule gives
\[
 \frac{\phi(w)+\exp(-1)}{(w+1)^2}
 \longrightarrow\frac{\exp(-1)}2
 \qquad(w\to-1,\ w\ne-1),
\]
because the differentiated quotient cancels to \(\exp(w)/2\) away from
\(-1\).  Composing with each branch, rewriting
\(W_j(z)\exp(W_j(z))=z\), and inverting the nonzero limit yields
\[
 \frac{(W_j(z)+1)^2}{z+\exp(-1)}\longrightarrow2\exp(1),
 \qquad j\in\{0,-1\}.
\]
Thus both squared displacements are asymptotic to
\(2\exp(1)(z+\exp(-1))\).  The branch signs select the square root, with no
sign ambiguity:
\[
 W_0(z)+1\sim+\mathcal S(z),
 \qquad
 W_{-1}(z)+1\sim-\mathcal S(z)
 \qquad(z\downarrow b).
\]

The complete public inventory of
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointAsymptotics.lean} is one noncomputable
definition and eight theorems:
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.51\textwidth}
                         >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.43\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endhead
\nolinkurl{lambertWBranchPointScale} & Definition of \(\sqrt{2\exp(1)(z+\exp(-1))}\). \\
\nolinkurl{lambertWBranchPointScale_pos} & Positivity for \(b<z\). \\
\nolinkurl{lambertWBranchPointScale_sq} & Exact scale-square identity for \(b<z\). \\
\nolinkurl{tendsto_principalLambertW_add_one_sq_div_branchPoint} & Principal squared-ratio limit \(2\exp(1)\). \\
\nolinkurl{tendsto_lowerLambertW_add_one_sq_div_branchPoint} & Lower squared-ratio limit \(2\exp(1)\). \\
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_add_one_sq_isEquivalent_branchPoint} & Principal squared-displacement equivalence. \\
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_add_one_sq_isEquivalent_branchPoint} & Lower squared-displacement equivalence. \\
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_add_one_isEquivalent_branchPoint} & Signed principal equivalence to \(+\mathcal S\). \\
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_add_one_isEquivalent_branchPoint} & Signed lower equivalence to \(-\mathcal S\). \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

This closes only the leading square-root (first Puiseux) equivalence.  No
\(O(\Delta)\) remainder, higher coefficient, or convergent/full Puiseux
expansion has been proved.  Named endpoint derivative, secant, and
square-root wrappers for the generic power--exponential phases or their
Fabius specializations also remain open, as does the generic
\(m\mathbin{\pm}\sqrt m\) threshold/sign/shape layer.
These two leaves are a current-tree tranche compiled on 30 August 2026:
the inventory is exactly eight public geometry theorems and one public
asymptotic definition plus eight public asymptotic theorems, excluding
private proof helpers.  They are recorded without inventing an aggregate
commit hash and are not attributed to the older title-page snapshot.

The reusable layer treats
\[
  S_{m,A,\beta}(\lambda)=A\lambda^m e^{-\beta\lambda},
  \qquad
  P_{m,A,\beta}
    =A\left(\frac m\beta e^{-1}\right)^m,
\]
under \(m\in\PositiveIntegers\), \(A>0\), and \(\beta>0\).
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertCalculus.lean} proves
\nolinkurl{principalPowerExponentialPhase_continuousOn_Icc} on
\([0,P_{m,A,\beta}]\) and
\nolinkurl{lowerPowerExponentialPhase_continuousOn_Ioc} on
\((0,P_{m,A,\beta}]\).  Both proofs compose the raw endpoint theorems with
the globally continuous normalized argument
\nolinkurl{powerExponentialLambertArgument_continuous}.  For \(x\) in the
latter interval and
\(\lambda\ge0\),
\nolinkurl{powerExponentialSaddle_eq_iff_eq_principal_or_eq_lower} is the
exact equivalence
\[
 S_{m,A,\beta}(\lambda)=x
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \lambda=\lambda_0(x)\ \text{or}\ \lambda=\lambda_{-1}(x).
\]
The two values are unequal when \(0<x<P_{m,A,\beta}\), by
\nolinkurl{principalPowerExponentialPhase_ne_lowerPowerExponentialPhase}.
Both classification results live in
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertInverse.lean}.  The hypothesis
\(\lambda\ge0\) matters when \(m\) is even, because negative inputs can
produce further positive profile values.

The next import,
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertAsymptotics.lean}, defines the intrinsic
scale
\[
 \epsilon(x)=\frac{\beta}{m}\left(\frac{x}{A}\right)^{1/m}
\]
and proves exactly
\[
 \lambda_0(x)\sim\left(\frac{x}{A}\right)^{1/m},\qquad
 \lambda_{-1}(x)\longrightarrow+\infty,\qquad
 \lambda_{-1}(x)-M(x)\longrightarrow0
 \quad(x\downarrow0),
\]
where
\[
 M(x)=-\frac m\beta
 \bigl(\log\epsilon(x)-\log|\log\epsilon(x)|\bigr).
\]
The declarations are
\nolinkurl{powerExponentialLambertEpsilon},
\nolinkurl{principalPowerExponentialPhase_isEquivalent_rpow},
\nolinkurl{tendsto_lowerPowerExponentialPhase_nhdsGT_zero_atTop},
\nolinkurl{lowerPowerExponentialPhaseIntrinsicMain}, and
\nolinkurl{lowerPowerExponentialPhase_sub_intrinsicMain_tendsto_zero}.
This generic result is intentionally only the intrinsic two-term
\(\epsilon\)-expansion with \(o(1)\) remainder.  A cleaned
\(L=\log(A/x)\) formula, additional terms or a complete series, a
named generic-phase branch-point vertical-tangent/secant wrapper, and a
transported leading square-root law remain outside this tranche.  The raw
branch-point statements above do not by themselves create those scaled
phase wrappers.

The separate \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertCurvature.lean} does supply
the generic regular-interior second derivative.  If \(\lambda\) denotes
either scaled branch and
\(0<x<P_{m,A,\beta}\), then, under the same hypotheses
\(m\in\PositiveIntegers\), \(A>0\), and \(\beta>0\),
\[
 \lambda''(x)=
 \frac{\lambda(x)\bigl(m-(m-\beta\lambda(x))^2\bigr)}
      {x^2\bigl(m-\beta\lambda(x)\bigr)^3}.
\]
The complete public surface of that module consists of the two
derivative-of-derivative witnesses
\nolinkurl{deriv_principalPowerExponentialPhase_hasDerivAt} and
\nolinkurl{deriv_lowerPowerExponentialPhase_hasDerivAt}, together with their
value corollaries
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_principalPowerExponentialPhase} and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerPowerExponentialPhase}.  The factorization
suggests candidate thresholds
\(\beta\lambda=m\mathbin{\pm}\sqrt m\), but generic sign, zero-set,
threshold, inflection, and strict-convexity/concavity packages have not been
formalized and are not claimed here.

Finally \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertFabius.lean} specializes
\((m,A,\beta)=(1,1,\log2)\).  It defines
\nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase}, identifies the other branch with
\nolinkurl{fabiusLambertPhase}, and exports
\nolinkurl{fabiusSaddle_eq_iff_eq_principal_or_eq_lower},
\nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_ne_fabiusLambertPhase},
\nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_continuousOn_Icc}, and
\nolinkurl{fabiusLambertPhase_continuousOn_Ioc}.
\nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_isEquivalent_id} gives the
specialized principal asymptotic \(\lambda_0(x)\sim x\); the generic lower
divergence and intrinsic expansion specialize to the existing lower Fabius
phase.

The curvature specialization is isolated in
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertFabiusCurvature.lean}.  Write
\[
 x_*:=\frac{2e^{-2}}{\log2},
 \qquad P:=\frac{e^{-1}}{\log2}.
\]
For the lower phase it proves \(x_*\in(0,P)\),
\(\lambda_{-1}(x_*)=2/\log2\), and, for \(0<x<P\),
\[
 \lambda_{-1}''(x)=
 \frac{(\log2)\lambda_{-1}(x)^2
       \bigl(2-(\log2)\lambda_{-1}(x)\bigr)}
      {x^2\bigl(1-(\log2)\lambda_{-1}(x)\bigr)^3}.
\]
Its second derivative is positive exactly when \(x<x_*\), negative exactly
when \(x_*<x\), and zero exactly when \(x=x_*\).  Thus \(x_*\) is the exact
formalized inflection input: the phase is strictly convex on
\((0,x_*]\) and strictly concave on \([x_*,P]\).

The principal specialization deliberately uses the raw-\(W_0\) curvature
API rather than restricting the generic positive-input formula.  Globally,
\[
 \lambda_0(x)=-\frac{W_0(-x\log2)}{\log2};
\]
for every \(x<P\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \lambda_0'(x)
   &=\frac{1}{e^{W_0(-x\log2)}(W_0(-x\log2)+1)},\\
 \lambda_0''(x)
   &=\frac{(\log2)e^{-2W_0(-x\log2)}(W_0(-x\log2)+2)}
           {(W_0(-x\log2)+1)^3}>0.
\end{aligned}
\]
In particular \(\lambda_0''(0)=2\log2\), and the phase is strictly convex
on the full endpoint-inclusive half-line \((-\infty,P]\), including all
negative inputs and zero.  The leaf's complete public surface is one
noncomputable definition and eighteen theorems:

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.08\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.47\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
No. & Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
No. & Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endhead
1 & \nolinkurl{fabiusLambertInflectionInput} & Definition of \(x_*=2e^{-2}/\log2\). \\
2 & \nolinkurl{fabiusLambertInflectionInput_mem_Ioo} & \(x_*\in(0,P)\). \\
3 & \nolinkurl{fabiusLambertPhase_inflectionInput} & \(\lambda_{-1}(x_*)=2/\log2\). \\
4 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase} & Exact lower-phase second derivative on \((0,P)\). \\
5 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_pos_iff} & For \(0<x<P\), \(0<\lambda_{-1}''(x)\iff x<x_*\). \\
6 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_neg_iff} & For \(0<x<P\), \(\lambda_{-1}''(x)<0\iff x_*<x\). \\
7 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_eq_zero_iff} & For \(0<x<P\), \(\lambda_{-1}''(x)=0\iff x=x_*\). \\
8 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_inflectionInput} & The simplified equality \(\lambda_{-1}''(x_*)=0\). \\
9 & \nolinkurl{strictConvexOn_fabiusLambertPhase_left} & Strict convexity on \(\operatorname{Ioc}(0,x_*)\). \\
10 & \nolinkurl{strictConcaveOn_fabiusLambertPhase_right} & Strict concavity on \(\operatorname{Icc}(x_*,P)\). \\
11 & \nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_eq_principalLambertW} & Global affine-\(W_0\) coordinate formula. \\
12 & \nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_continuousOn_Iic} & Continuity on \(\operatorname{Iic}(P)\). \\
13 & \nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_hasDerivAt} & First-derivative witness for every \(x<P\). \\
14 & \nolinkurl{deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase} & Exact first-derivative value for every \(x<P\). \\
15 & \nolinkurl{deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_hasDerivAt} & Derivative-of-derivative witness for every \(x<P\). \\
16 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase} & Exact principal second-derivative value for every \(x<P\). \\
17 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_pos} & Strict positivity for every \(x<P\). \\
18 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_zero} & The simplified equality \(\lambda_0''(0)=2\log2\). \\
19 & \nolinkurl{strictConvexOn_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase} & Strict convexity on \(\operatorname{Iic}(P)\). \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

The import ladder reflects the proof structure:
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambert.lean} imports the raw lower and principal
branches, then
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertCalculus.lean},
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertInverse.lean}, and
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertAsymptotics.lean} add continuity,
classification, and asymptotics in that order.
\nolinkurl{PrincipalLambertW.lean} itself imports
\nolinkurl{LowerLambertW.lean};
\nolinkurl{LambertBranchDichotomy.lean} imports the principal leaf; and
\nolinkurl{LambertWCurvature.lean} imports that pair layer.
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointGeometry.lean} then imports exactly the raw
curvature leaf, and
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointAsymptotics.lean} imports the geometry leaf
together with Mathlib's specific-asymptotics and L'H\^opital modules.  Thus
the raw dependency order is
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{lower}\longrightarrow\text{principal}\longrightarrow
 \text{branch dichotomy}\longrightarrow\text{curvature}\\
 {}\longrightarrow\text{branch-point geometry}\longrightarrow
 \text{branch-point asymptotics}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertCurvature.lean} imports the generic
calculus layer and contains only the four branch-generic second-derivative
declarations above.  The non-curvature Fabius bridge imports the asymptotic
layer together with the older generalized-coordinate and Fabius saddle
modules, while
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertFabiusCurvature.lean} imports that bridge
and both curvature leaves.  The umbrella \nolinkurl{FabiusFunction.lean}
lists \nolinkurl{LambertWCurvature.lean},
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointGeometry.lean}, and
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointAsymptotics.lean} consecutively in dependency
order before the generic power--exponential leaves; it also exposes the two
scaled curvature modules.  These lemmas support the corrected
comparisons in both the k-fold project and the principal Fabius saddle
analysis.

\subsection{Decay comparisons}
\nolinkurl{FabiusFlatness.lean} and \nolinkurl{FabiusDecayComparison.lean} convert the log-squared law into two useful endpoint comparisons.  For every \(m>0\),
\[
  F(x)=o(x^m)
  \qquad(x\downarrow0),
\]
so the function is smaller than every power.  On the other hand, for every \(c>0\),
\[
  e^{-c/x}=o(F(x)),
\]
so it is much larger than an \(e^{-c/x}\) flat function.

Taking logarithms reduces these statements to comparing \((\log(1/x))^2\) with \(\log(1/x)\) and with \(1/x\).  The code packages positivity and eventual monotonicity so the exponential comparison can use standard filter lemmas.

\section{Recurring proof-engineering patterns}\label{ch:engineering}

\subsection{Strong induction as the default arithmetic engine}
Many sequences in the project depend on all smaller indices, not only on the predecessor.  The idiomatic proof begins with strong induction:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pseudo]
refine Nat.strong_induction_on n ?_
intro n ih
-- ih : forall m < n, P m
\end{lstlisting}
A finite sum over \nolinkurl{Finset.range n} then supplies the inequality needed to invoke \nolinkurl{ih}.  It is often worth proving a helper lemma that converts membership in the range directly into the strict inequality expected by the hypothesis.

Strong induction also matches the highest-bit evaluator, whose recursive remainder is smaller but not necessarily one less.  Using the same well-founded order for definition and correctness proof keeps the program and theorem structurally aligned.

\subsection{Finite sums and index transport}
The repository moves frequently among:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \nolinkurl{Finset.range n};
  \item subtype indices \nolinkurl{Fin n};
  \item integer intervals;
  \item list positions; and
  \item antidiagonals describing coefficient convolution.
\end{itemize}
Rather than rewriting all indices inside a large expression, the code usually proves an equivalence between finite index types and invokes a reindexing theorem.  A clean bijection proof has four parts: the forward map, inverse map, membership preservation, and left/right inverse equalities.

For even/odd splits, a specialized theorem is especially valuable:
\[
  \sum_{j<2N}f(j)
  =\sum_{k<N}f(2k)+\sum_{k<N}f(2k+1).
\]
Once established, it drives Thue--Morse identities, convolution recurrences, and parity counts.

\subsection{Casts as explicit interfaces}
Exact theorems live in \(\NaturalNumbers\), \(\IntegerNumbers\), or \(\RationalNumbers\); analytic theorems live in \(\RealNumbers\) or \(\ComplexNumbers\).  Lean will insert canonical casts, but it will not silently prove that a finite sum commutes with the cast or that a natural exponent has the intended target type.

The code therefore proves and reuses lemmas of the forms
\begin{align*}
  \left(\sum_{k\in s}q_k:\RationalNumbers\right)\!\uparrow^{\RealNumbers}
    &=\sum_{k\in s}(q_k:\RealNumbers),\\
  (2^n:\RationalNumbers)\!\uparrow^{\RealNumbers}&=(2:\RealNumbers)^n,\\
  \left(\binom nk:\NaturalNumbers\right)\!\uparrow^{\RationalNumbers}
    &=\binom nk \text{ interpreted in }\RationalNumbers.
\end{align*}
In actual Lean syntax the upward arrows are ordinary coercions.  Tactics such as \nolinkurl{norm_cast}, \nolinkurl{exact_mod_cast}, and \nolinkurl{push_cast} are used after the expression is put in a form they recognize.

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Lean pattern.}\space
Do not ask \nolinkurl{norm_cast} to solve a goal containing unreduced divisions, nested sums, and conditionals.  First rewrite the exact recurrence, move casts through finite sums, simplify powers, and only then invoke cast normalization.  The repository's helper lemmas reflect this staged approach.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Pointwise derivatives instead of raw \texttt{deriv}}
A theorem of type \nolinkurl{HasDerivAt f f' x} carries both differentiability and the derivative value.  It composes under addition, multiplication, affine maps, logarithms, and exponentials.  By contrast, an equality involving \nolinkurl{deriv f x} often creates a side goal proving \nolinkurl{DifferentiableAt f x} before it can be rewritten.

Accordingly, the calculus modules first prove \nolinkurl{HasDerivAt} identities and derive \nolinkurl{deriv} corollaries only when a source theorem is stated in that notation.  Iterated derivatives are handled through \nolinkurl{iteratedDeriv} or \nolinkurl{ContDiff} lemmas with explicit order parameters.

\subsection{Piecewise functions and boundary glue}
The bounded function, fixed-point transform, splines, and endpoint representatives are all piecewise.  The source follows a stable pattern:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item define each branch as a total function;
  \item prove branch continuity or differentiability;
  \item prove equality of branch values at the boundary;
  \item apply a piecewise glue theorem; and
  \item derive simplified branch lemmas for later rewriting.
\end{enumerate}
The simplified lemmas are often tagged for the simplifier only when their conditions are syntactically obvious.  Overly aggressive \nolinkurl{simp} tags on overlapping branches can create loops or choose an unintended normal form.

\subsection{Local finiteness before infinite-sum algebra}
For translate sums, the code first proves finite support at a point or on a neighborhood.  Only then does it reindex or differentiate a \nolinkurl{tsum}.  This is safer than invoking general unconditional summability theorems on a series whose convergence is really trivial by support.

A typical theorem states that the summand is zero outside a finite integer interval.  From this it derives summability, rewrites the \nolinkurl{tsum} as a \nolinkurl{Finset.sum}, performs the algebra, and converts back only if the public theorem uses \nolinkurl{tsum} notation.

\subsection{Integrability from support and bounds}
The most common integrability proof has three ingredients:
\begin{itemize}
  \item continuity or measurability of the integrand;
  \item compact support; and
  \item a uniform bound on that support.
\end{itemize}
The repository builds reusable support lemmas for \(F\), \(\RvachevUp\), their derivatives, and affine transforms.  Polynomial or exponential multipliers remain bounded on a compact support, so moment and transform integrability follows from a generic compact-support theorem.

This style avoids global estimates that are stronger than needed.  For instance, \(x^n\RvachevUp(x)\) is integrable not because \(x^n\) is globally bounded, but because the product vanishes outside \([-1,1]\).

\subsection{Interchanging sums, limits, derivatives, and integrals}
Every interchange in the project is supported by a named convergence theorem:
\begin{itemize}
  \item finite sums commute with everything by algebra;
  \item infinite sums commute with integrals under a summable integrable majorant;
  \item limits pass under integrals by dominated or bounded convergence;
  \item derivatives pass through series under local uniform convergence of derivative series; and
  \item contour limits use explicit uniform tail bounds.
\end{itemize}
The proof normally constructs the majorant first, proves its summability or integrability, and only then invokes the interchange theorem.  This order makes failures local: if an informal series is not uniformly controlled, the missing hypothesis appears before the main identity is attempted.

\subsection{Big-O, little-o, and eventual domains}
Asymptotic modules use filters rather than epsilon notation.  A theorem of the form
\[
  f(x)=O(g(x))\quad(x\to0^+)
\]
is represented through \nolinkurl{IsBigO} at a within-neighborhood filter.  The proof often begins with
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pseudo]
filter_upwards [eventually_pos, eventually_small] with x hxpos hxsmall
\end{lstlisting}
and establishes a pointwise norm inequality.

Eventual positivity is particularly important for logarithms, divisions, and Lambert phases.  The source proves threshold lemmas once and then includes them in filter-upwards blocks.  Transitivity of Big-O and equivalence under asymptotically equal scales are used heavily to transfer results from the exact phase \(\lambda(x)\) to \(-\log x\).

\subsection{Polynomial identities}
Exact finite identities are proved at the polynomial level whenever possible.  The usual techniques are:
\begin{itemize}
  \item extensionality by coefficients;
  \item evaluation at an arbitrary point followed by \nolinkurl{ring};
  \item induction using polynomial addition and multiplication; and
  \item degree bounds plus a roots theorem.
\end{itemize}
For translation-invariant Thue--Morse sums, a degree bound and finite-difference theorem often give a cleaner proof than expanding every binomial coefficient.

\subsection{Choosing automation deliberately}
The development uses automation, but proofs are structured so each tactic sees an appropriate goal:
\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=2.5em]
  \item[\texttt{simp}] unfolds branch lemmas, finite-range membership, and standard algebraic identities.
  \item[\texttt{norm\_num}] closes concrete rational and natural computations, especially counterexamples and base cases.
  \item[\texttt{ring} / \texttt{ring\_nf}] handles polynomial identities after divisions and casts have been normalized.
  \item[\texttt{linarith} / \texttt{nlinarith}] combines real inequalities once transcendental terms have been abstracted as variables with known bounds.
  \item[\texttt{positivity}] proves positivity of products, powers, factorials, exponentials, and denominators.
  \item[\texttt{omega}] handles Presburger arithmetic for indices, ranges, and exponents.
  \item[\texttt{field\_simp}] clears rational or field denominators after nonzero hypotheses are established.
  \item[\texttt{aesop}] resolves local structural goals, but is not used as a substitute for choosing the main induction or analytic theorem.
\end{description}

\subsection{Public wrappers and internal strength}
Theorem-producing modules tend to prove results in a strong reusable form: unrestricted numerators, arbitrary candidate functions, general coefficient rings, or explicit error constants.  Paper-facing wrappers then add notation and specialize hypotheses.

When extending the library, search for the strongest internal theorem before reproving a source-shaped special case.  Names ending in \nolinkurl{_all}, \nolinkurl{_global}, \nolinkurl{_scalar}, \nolinkurl{_correct}, \nolinkurl{_transfer}, or \nolinkurl{_of_isFabius} often signal the reusable layer.

\subsection{No placeholders and explicit corrections}
The repository states that the development builds without \nolinkurl{sorry}.  More importantly, corrected claims are not represented by axioms that merely assume the missing fact.  When a source formula is false, the project either adds the necessary hypothesis, changes the object to the signed extension, weakens the error term, or proves a counterexample.

This makes the dependency graph epistemically meaningful.  A theorem about the Fabius function ultimately reduces to mathlib, Lean's kernel, explicit definitions, and proved lemmas---not to a paper citation embedded as an axiom.

\section{How to read and extend the code}\label{ch:extending}

\subsection{Five recommended reading paths}
Different goals call for different entry points.

\subsubsection{Path A: existence and uniqueness}
Read, in order:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \nolinkurl{Basic.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{Differential.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{DyadicClosedForm.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic.lean}; and
  \item \nolinkurl{Existence.lean}.
\end{enumerate}
This path explains the abstract specification, generic derivative consequences, dyadic rigidity, and fixed-point implementation.

\subsubsection{Path B: exact evaluation}
Read:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \nolinkurl{Arithmetic.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{DyadicCorrectness.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{DyadicClosedForm.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic.lean}; and
  \item \nolinkurl{GlobalDyadic.lean}.
\end{enumerate}
Keep an editor evaluation buffer open and inspect the types of table, Horner, and wrapper correctness theorems.

\subsubsection{Path C: the historical Rvachev theory}
Read:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \nolinkurl{GlobalExtension.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FourierProduct.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FourierAnalytic.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{OriginalUniqueness.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{ProbabilityRepresentation.lean}; and
  \item \nolinkurl{StepApproximationLimit.lean}.
\end{enumerate}
This path emphasizes compact support, transform refinement, convolutions, and pointwise approximants.

\subsubsection{Path D (post-snapshot): integer and fractional Volterra calculus}
The fractional modules in this path were included in the compiled full-facade
checkpoint \nolinkurl{22f801337}.
Read:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \nolinkurl{NormalizedVolterra.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FractionalVolterra.lean} and
        \nolinkurl{FractionalVolterraSemigroup.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FractionalVolterraCalculus.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{GlobalExtension.lean} and
        \nolinkurl{FabiusAntiderivatives.lean}; and
  \item \nolinkurl{FabiusFractionalVolterra.lean}.
\end{enumerate}
The first module is the reusable natural-order layer: arbitrary base points and oriented
endpoints, real normed-space-valued kernels and algebra, exact affine covariance
for every real scale, arbitrary base-point shifts, and the zero-local-tail Taylor
jet.  For continuous Banach-valued inputs it additionally identifies literal
iteration with the direct kernel and supplies the semigroup's strict FTC,
derivative cancellation, smoothness, and Taylor reconstruction.
\nolinkurl{FractionalVolterra.lean} and its semigroup companion give the
Gamma-normalized real-order operator and positive-order composition on ordered
intervals.  \nolinkurl{FractionalVolterraCalculus.lean} adds positive affine
covariance for arbitrary real order and, for \(\alpha>0\), the exact
integration-by-parts order-raising law with its left-endpoint boundary term.
The two Fabius-specific modules are used only after this generic work:
\nolinkurl{FabiusAntiderivatives.lean} gives the signed-global and bounded
